		<item>
		<title>How TCS built an agentic test orchestrator with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-tcs-built-an-agentic-test-orchestrator-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Varun Valsan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 10 Nov 2026 16:59:43 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">78fec7a3d16c37b034f25f58b80fa621d5222843</guid>

					<description>Tata Consultancy Services (TCS) built an agentic test orchestrator on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore that turns requirements into executable, governed tests for its Banking, Financial Services, and Insurance (BFSI) clients. Learn how specialized agents, per-tenant isolation, and workload-aware execution cut testing effort by 88% in an internal benchmark.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;This post was co-written with Varun Valsan from Tata Consultancy Services (TCS).&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tata Consultancy Services (TCS) runs large-scale testing for Banking, Financial Services, and Insurance (BFSI) clients, where the slowest part of a release is rarely writing application code. The larger constraint is the chain of work that follows: interpreting requirements, designing scenarios, writing and maintaining automation, preparing execution environments, investigating failures, and assembling evidence for review.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;BFSI clients need to increase testing throughput while preserving the controls expected in financial services. The objective wasn’t to replace quality engineers. It was to remove repetitive translation and execution work so that engineers could spend more time on risk analysis, exploratory testing, and release decisions. These industry needs generated four specific requirements for TCS. First, every test must be grounded in project-specific context rather than generic prompts. Second, it had to cover both API and browser-based UI testing to match how BFSI applications are built. Third, security is paramount, so every tenant’s data and knowledge store must be isolated. Finally, it must provide a repeatable path from requirement ingestion to test evidence and reporting, so the gains hold across teams and releases. In this post, we show how TCS built an agentic test orchestrator that turns requirements into executable, governed tests, and what it changed for its BFSI testing teams.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-conventional-automation-and-systems-were-not-enough"&gt;Why conventional automation and systems weren’t enough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;TCS already uses traditional test automation for its clients, and it does improve execution speed. But it leaves the slow work untouched: teams must still create the scenarios, convert them into scripts, maintain locators and dependencies, and diagnose failures. When a linked user story changes, scripts generated for an earlier story can quickly become stale. A single generative AI prompt also doesn’t provide the control needed for a multi-stage engineering workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;TCS therefore designed the Agentic Test Orchestrator to decompose testing into specialized tasks. Each task has its own agent, tools, validation rules, and context. A deterministic supervisor routes work, while an execution workflow controls retries, error classification, persistence, and reporting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To run these agents, TCS chose &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;, a service to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. Three capabilities drove the decision. First, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; offers a choice of models, so each agent uses the model that fits its task, such as scenario design or code generation, and TCS can adopt newer models as they become available. Second, AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, scales with demand, so testers across many tenants can run generation and execution sessions at the same time without TCS provisioning or tuning agent servers. Third, the runtime runs each session in its own isolated microVM, which gives TCS session-level separation for its multi-tenant platform without having to build that layer itself. Together, these capabilities let TCS focus its engineering effort on testing logic rather than agent infrastructure, which shortened the time to bring the platform to its BFSI clients.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the end-to-end flow of the platform across five stages, with a per-tenant security layer underneath. Inputs from Jira, business requirement documents (BRDs), and GitLab enter a tenant web application that runs on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eks/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS)&lt;/a&gt; behind &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudfront/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudFront&lt;/a&gt;, with sign-in through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cognito/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Cognito&lt;/a&gt;. TCS uses Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime to host the platform’s specialized agents, built with the &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents SDK&lt;/a&gt;. These agents generate the tests, refine them with the tester, and manage their execution. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ecs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS)&lt;/a&gt; runs the generated tests. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon DynamoDB&lt;/a&gt; store the results, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt; captures the logs. Throughout, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/waf/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS WAF&lt;/a&gt;, Amazon Cognito, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/kms/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS)&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/secrets-manager/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Secrets Manager&lt;/a&gt; enforce per-tenant security and isolation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-20381-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-20381-1.png" alt="Architecture of the TCS Agentic Test Orchestrator showing the five-stage flow and per-tenant security layer on AWS" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: High-level solution architecture&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The TCS Agentic Test Orchestrator is a multi-tenant software as a service (SaaS) solution built on AWS. A tester provides requirements through Jira stories, business requirement documents (BRDs), Swagger specifications, uploaded documents, GitLab source folders, or captured UI flows. From those inputs the platform then generates test scenarios, Gherkin feature files, and executable Java or Python tests. It runs those tests on Amazon ECS, stores artifacts and results, and shows progress and reports in a per-tenant web application. The sections that follow describe how each part of the platform meets one of the four requirements TCS set.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="separate-the-control-plane-from-each-tenant-application-plane"&gt;1. Separate the control plane from each tenant application plane&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;TCS required strict isolation of each tenant’s identity, data, and keys, so the platform uses pooled compute with siloed data and identity. A shared control plane provides the Amazon EKS cluster, container registry, administrative portal, tenant registry, onboarding automation, Amazon CloudFront distributions, and AWS WAF protection. Each tenant application plane receives its own Kubernetes namespace, Amazon Cognito user pool, AWS KMS key, Amazon S3 bucket, Amazon DynamoDB tables, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/a&gt;. This separation gives each BFSI tenant the data and key boundaries that financial-services controls demand.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tenant onboarding is event driven. The administrative API writes a pending tenant record, emits an event through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eventbridge/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon EventBridge&lt;/a&gt;, and starts an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/codebuild/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CodeBuild&lt;/a&gt; project that deploys the tenant infrastructure with AWS Cloud Development Kit (&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cdk/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CDK&lt;/a&gt;). This pattern makes tenant provisioning repeatable while retaining tenant-specific identity and data boundaries that TCS needed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="ground-every-agent-in-project-context"&gt;2. Ground every agent in project context&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;TCS’s first requirement was that generation reflect each project’s domain rather than a generic prompt, so grounding is built into the platform. The platform stores uploaded requirements and generated artifacts in Amazon S3 and session metadata in Amazon DynamoDB. Project documents and synchronized source files are indexed in Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases using vector embeddings. Before material reaches an agent, retrieval is filtered by tenant, project, and session context, so one client’s context doesn’t inform another’s tests.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This context layer supports the many ways a BFSI team expresses requirements: Jira stories and linked story chains, BRDs, Swagger definitions, reference code, locator documents, setup modules, test data, and GitLab source folders. For UI automation, a separate capture service uses Puppeteer to convert a URL and a natural-language goal into a UI flow document and an XPath locator document.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="use-specialized-agents-instead-of-one-large-prompt"&gt;3. Use specialized agents instead of one large prompt&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because a single prompt can’t give a regulated workflow the control it needs, the platform divides the work among specialized agents that run as containerized services in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime using the Strands Agents SDK. A generation runtime routes requests to dedicated scenario, Gherkin, code-generation, and validation tools. Additional runtimes support conversational refinement, BRD-to-story conversion, incremental generation across linked Jira stories, and test execution. Each agent owns a stage of the lifecycle. Here’s a brief overview of each specialized agent:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Generation agent:&lt;/strong&gt; Creates structured scenarios, converts them to Gherkin, generates Java or Python automation, and validates generated code.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Chat agent:&lt;/strong&gt; Classifies a request as an information query or an update request, then answers or revises the active artifact.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Dynamic task agent:&lt;/strong&gt; Retrieves BRD context and produces a structured user story with requirements and acceptance criteria.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Incremental agent:&lt;/strong&gt; Extends scenarios, Gherkin, or code across a linked Jira story chain instead of starting from scratch.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Execution agent:&lt;/strong&gt; Fetches artifacts, chooses an execution environment, runs tests, classifies failures, applies controlled fixes, and saves final results.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="route-api-and-ui-tests-to-the-right-execution-environment"&gt;4. Route API and UI tests to the right execution environment&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;TCS needed to cover both API and browser-based UI testing, which place different demands on infrastructure, so the execution agent detects the test type from the generated code and routes it accordingly. API tests are routed to Amazon ECS on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fargate/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Fargate&lt;/a&gt;. Selenium UI tests are routed to an Amazon ECS cluster backed by &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2)&lt;/a&gt;, where the executor can run Chrome with a virtual display.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For UI runs, the executor can capture the virtual display with FFmpeg and publish an HTTP Live Streaming (HLS) stream to Amazon S3 for delivery through Amazon CloudFront. Test logs are sent to Amazon CloudWatch, and generated Cucumber reports are stored in Amazon S3 with time-limited access.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="control-the-execution-and-correction-loop"&gt;5. Control the execution and correction loop&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To keep automated correction safe enough for a BFSI pipeline, the execution agent defines its fetch, execute, fix, and save steps as a &lt;a href="https://www.langchain.com/langgraph" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LangGraph&lt;/a&gt; workflow. LangGraph’s explicit state graph gives TCS a fixed step order and a bounded retry loop, while AgentCore runtime hosts, scales, and isolates the agent that runs it. The fetch node retrieves the test project and generated assets, sanitizes files, and detects the framework. The execute node launches the selected ECS task, monitors status, retrieves logs, and parses results.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a failure is classified as a code error and the retry policy allows it, a fixing agent receives a constrained error context and proposes a targeted correction. Safety checks reject empty files, unexpected new files, or extreme changes in file size. Environment errors, such as connectivity or authentication failures, are not sent to the code fixer. The save node persists the final code, status, attempts, logs, parsed results, and report location.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="security-and-operational-controls"&gt;6. Security and operational controls&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because the solution serves regulated BFSI tenants, it applies controls at the identity, network, data, and application layers. For identity, separate Amazon Cognito user pools serve platform administrators and each tenant, with backend JSON Web Token (JWT) validation and role-based authorization. For data, each tenant has its own AWS KMS keys and encrypted Amazon S3 storage, DynamoDB access is tenant-scoped, and AWS Secrets Manager holds Jira and GitLab credentials. At the workload level on Amazon EKS, Kubernetes namespaces, NetworkPolicy, and ResourceQuota separate tenants, and containers run as non-root with dropped Linux capabilities. At the network edge, AWS WAF managed rules and request-rate controls protect the platform. Traffic is delivered over HTTPS through Amazon CloudFront and the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/elasticloadbalancing/application-load-balancer/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Application Load Balancer&lt;/a&gt;, and responses carry restrictive security headers. Across all of these, OpenTelemetry traces capture agent activity while Amazon CloudWatch logs and Container Insights provide application and execution observability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="outcomes"&gt;Outcomes&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;TCS runs the Agentic Test Orchestrator in production for its BFSI clients. To quantify the effort savings, TCS measured the solution against its existing process on a representative BFSI workload of about 100 test scenarios. In the manual process, each scenario takes roughly two hours of engineer effort to design, script, and execute, or about 200 hours for the full set. With the Agentic Test Orchestrator, the average effort was approximately 14 minutes per scenario, bringing the same workload to about 24 hours. This represents an 88 percent reduction in effort in this comparison. (&lt;em&gt;These figures come from a controlled internal benchmark that TCS ran across approximately 100 scenarios. Actual results vary by workload, application complexity, and team.)&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For TCS, the effort number matters less than the operating model behind it. Requirement ingestion, generation, execution, correction, and reporting now form one traceable workflow instead of a chain of disconnected handoffs. Quality engineers review and govern AI-produced artifacts rather than hand-building them, which returns their time to the risk analysis, exploratory testing, and release decisions that the manual process crowded out. That is the outcome TCS set out to reach.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The problem TCS set out to solve was not simply slow test execution. It was the fragmented, manual path from business requirements to trustworthy test evidence. This is the part of a BFSI release that consumes engineering time without adding assurance. In this post, we showed how TCS built the Agentic Test Orchestrator on AWS to close that gap by combining contextual retrieval, specialized agents, workload-aware execution, isolated per-tenant services, and observable infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The result is a reusable pattern for applying generative AI to software quality engineering without reducing testing to a single prompt. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore hosts and scales the agents, Amazon Bedrock and Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases supply the AI capabilities and project context, Amazon EKS hosts the per-tenant application layer, Amazon ECS provides fit-for-purpose execution, and AWS security, storage, integration, and observability services support the full lifecycle. For TCS’s BFSI teams, the payoff is a faster, more consistent testing process that returns engineers’ time to risk, quality, and release decisions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more and deploy the solution in your own environment, visit the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/marketplace/pp/prodview-ivev3fu5js5bu" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;TCS Agentic Test Orchestrator in AWS Marketplace&lt;/a&gt;. If you would like help applying this pattern to your own testing workflows, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/contact-us/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;contact the AWS team&lt;/a&gt;. Have questions or a similar use case? Share them in the comments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Amazon Bedrock tokenomics data lake</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-bedrock-tokenomics-data-lake/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Steph Gooch]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 10 Nov 2026 16:56:36 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">fcb44d913a26d1a7b901b41bae672561748f42ff</guid>

					<description>Running Amazon Bedrock across many accounts shows you the bill but not what happened inside each call. This post builds a centralized observability data lake that aggregates Bedrock invocation logs from every linked account into one queryable table, so you can see prompts, token counts, model choice, and per-invocation cost across your organization.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;If you need an observability data lake for your &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; workloads, you’re not alone. When you’re running thousands of model invocations a day across multiple accounts, after a request is sent you might be wondering, what happened? You can see the aggregate bill, but you can’t see what happened inside each call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You might start to ask questions: what prompt was sent, what the model returned, how many tokens it used, which model handled it, and whether the response was any good! If you’re a platform or machine learning (ML) engineer looking after a fleet of Amazon Bedrock workloads across teams, that’s a real problem. You can’t debug a spike, you can’t spot wasteful prompts, and you can’t tell one team’s usage from another’s.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We built something to help: a centralized observability data lake for your Amazon Bedrock invocations. In this post, I cover what native data you have and how we designed the lake. I also show how to use it to see your prompts and derive the per-invocation cost. The &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cloudformation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CloudFormation&lt;/a&gt; templates, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lambda/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Lambda&lt;/a&gt; code, and SQL queries are all in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-token-cost-allocation" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Sample Bedrock Invocation Data Lake GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;. This post walks through the architecture and deployment steps. The repo has everything you need to deploy it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-amazon-bedrock-visibility-do-you-have-today"&gt;What Amazon Bedrock visibility do you have today?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock offers &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-invocation-logging.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;model invocation logging&lt;/a&gt;, and it’s genuinely powerful. You turn on invocation logs in the &lt;a href="https://us-east-1.console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/home?region=us-east-1#/settings" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;settings in each AWS account you are using Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;. It delivers a full record of requests and responses to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/pm/serv-s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The data exists, but it’s locked inside individual accounts. What you really want is a single, fast, queryable view of every invocation across your whole organization. That’s what the data lake gives you.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-is-the-amazon-bedrock-observability-data-lake"&gt;What is the Amazon Bedrock observability data lake?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A centralized &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-invocation-logging.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock invocation log&lt;/a&gt; data lake pulls raw model invocation logs from your linked accounts into &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt;. Subscription log filters then forward those records to your central account. The central account processes the data through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/firehose/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Data Firehose&lt;/a&gt;, and exposes them through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/athena/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Athena&lt;/a&gt; as one clean table you can query across your organization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-21645-1.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-21645-1.jpg" alt="Architecture of the Bedrock invocation data lake spanning linked source accounts and a central account" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Amazon Bedrock invocation observability data lake architecture across a central account and linked accounts&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Everything is deployed as Infrastructure as Code using two &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSCloudFormation/latest/UserGuide/Welcome.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CloudFormation&lt;/a&gt; templates, so you can roll it out repeatably across every account in your organization. This can be found in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-token-cost-allocation" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Sample Bedrock Invocation Data Lake GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-does-each-bedrock-invocation-log-capture"&gt;What does each Amazon Bedrock invocation log capture?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After we run this solution, you will have a combined set of data across the linked account you deploy to so you can query with Athena. What you get per invocation after the logs are flattened into the processed table:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The prompt&lt;/strong&gt; the user or application sent.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The model response&lt;/strong&gt; that came back.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Input and output token counts&lt;/strong&gt;, so you can measure the true weight of each call.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The model&lt;/strong&gt; used, so you can understand utilization of models across teams.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The stop reason&lt;/strong&gt; (for example, &lt;code&gt;max_tokens&lt;/code&gt;), which tells you when a response was cut off.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The account and timestamp&lt;/strong&gt;, so you can attribute and time-slice everything.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/?trk=40032cd2-744c-4b2c-ad0c-cf60894e0921&amp;amp;sc_channel=ps&amp;amp;ef_id=CjwKCAjws_DTBhB_EiwAXZknGfru2_JWQb1nqidRQ4APsbkaGf1W2ywetPh_unJq1IEvVAlNJfLWzRoCpg4QAvD_BwE&amp;amp;gads_camp=23528572748&amp;amp;gads_ag=198422384371&amp;amp;gads_ad=795811958070&amp;amp;gads_kw=iam&amp;amp;gads_matchtype=e&amp;amp;gads_network=g&amp;amp;gads_device=c&amp;amp;gads_geo=9198785&amp;amp;gad_campaignid=23528572748&amp;amp;gbraid=0AAAAADjHtp-pAfh6ucw0EYbg13-jUO8NM&amp;amp;gclid=CjwKCAjws_DTBhB_EiwAXZknGfru2_JWQb1nqidRQ4APsbkaGf1W2ywetPh_unJq1IEvVAlNJfLWzRoCpg4QAvD_BwE" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) role&lt;/a&gt;, so you can see who sent the prompt.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After every invocation is captured like this, you go from “We used X tokens on Amazon Bedrock” to “Here is exactly what each model calls contained, and how efficient it was.” That’s the visibility platform and ML teams have been missing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-is-the-bedrock-data-lake-designed"&gt;How is the Amazon Bedrock data lake designed?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The data lake moves each invocation through five stages, from the moment Amazon Bedrock writes a log to the point you can query it in Athena.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="bedrock-writes-an-invocation-log"&gt;1. Amazon Bedrock writes an invocation log&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every time an application calls an Amazon Bedrock model, Amazon Bedrock can record what happened: which model, which account, which IAM identity made the call, how many input and output tokens were used, and the prompt and response text. You turn this on once per account with a single API call, pointing Amazon Bedrock at a CloudWatch log group and giving it a role that can write there.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="cloudwatch-forwards-the-logs-to-a-central-account"&gt;2. CloudWatch forwards the logs to a central account&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each source account gets a subscription filter on its Amazon Bedrock log group. Think of it as a permanent forwarding rule that says “send every new event to this address.” The address is an Amazon CloudWatch Logs destination living in your central account. Source accounts don’t get permissions on the central pipeline. They hand events to the destination, and the central account’s own role takes it from there.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="a-small-lambda-tidies-each-record"&gt;3. A small Lambda tidies each record&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;CloudWatch doesn’t hand over clean JSON. It bundles events into batches and wraps them compressed and encoded, so what arrives looks like gibberish until you unwrap it. A small Lambda function does three jobs:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Unwraps the batch back into individual log records.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Throws out the noise. CloudWatch sends occasional housekeeping messages and plain-English notices like “Permissions are correctly set for Amazon Bedrock logs.” These aren’t invocation data, and letting them through breaks the next stage.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Flattens one awkward field. The caller’s identity arrives nested one level down. The Lambda lifts it to the top so it’s straightforward to query later.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="amazon-data-firehose-converts-to-parquet-and-writes-to-s3"&gt;4. Amazon Data Firehose converts to Parquet and writes to S3&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Data Firehose buffers the records and writes them to an Amazon S3 bucket, converting from JSON to Parquet on the way through. Firehose also files each object under a date path (&lt;code&gt;year=2026/month=08/day=27/&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="use-athena-to-query-the-data-and-connect-it-to-cost-data"&gt;5. Use Athena to query the data and connect it to cost data&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/glue/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Glue&lt;/a&gt; table tells Athena how to read those files, so you can query the lake with ordinary SQL. You can see which users are using the most tokens, which models are commonly selected, and what people are prompting. If you want, you can also connect this to the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/aws-cost-management/aws-cost-and-usage-reporting/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Cost and Usage Report&lt;/a&gt; (CUR) to get the spend of user prompts by day.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-do-you-need-before-you-start"&gt;What do you need before you start?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A few things need to be in place before you deploy:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cli/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI)&lt;/a&gt; configured with credentials for each account.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;CloudFormation&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;CAPABILITY_IAM&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;CAPABILITY_NAMED_IAM&lt;/code&gt; permissions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;At least one linked account&lt;/strong&gt; (the account running your Amazon Bedrock workloads) and &lt;strong&gt;one central account&lt;/strong&gt; to receive the replicated logs (these can be the same account).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Python 3 with Boto3&lt;/strong&gt; (&lt;code&gt;pip install boto3&lt;/code&gt;), for the end-to-end test script.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-token-cost-allocation" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cloned repo&lt;/a&gt; with all the resources in it.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-do-you-deploy-it"&gt;How do you deploy it?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The order matters here.&lt;/strong&gt; The central account stack has to exist before the linked account stacks can reference it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="stage-1-deploy-the-central-data-lake-stack"&gt;Stage 1: Deploy the central data lake stack&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deploy the central CloudFormation stack first, into the account that will own the pipeline. Note two values from the stack outputs before moving on: the CloudWatch Logs destination ARN (which every source account needs) and the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/?trk=cb69a790-31b4-4f3f-ab38-8c131506ae2b&amp;amp;sc_channel=ps&amp;amp;ef_id=Cj0KCQjw2OnUBhC2ARIsACKyfaHOyrI2BSqC3eo7DZbbj0Ks030dwANVC-ITwsJaw_YzyJqknqgei6IaAm3WEALw_wcB:G:s&amp;amp;gads_camp=23528573981&amp;amp;gads_ag=191423962694&amp;amp;gads_ad=806967564154&amp;amp;gads_kw=amazon%20quick&amp;amp;gads_matchtype=e&amp;amp;gads_network=g&amp;amp;gads_device=c&amp;amp;gads_geo=1006993&amp;amp;gad_campaignid=23528573981&amp;amp;gbraid=0AAAAADjHtp_o1ijSErUcY81biixQPaoFW&amp;amp;gclid=Cj0KCQjw2OnUBhC2ARIsACKyfaHOyrI2BSqC3eo7DZbbj0Ks030dwANVC-ITwsJaw_YzyJqknqgei6IaAm3WEALw_wcB" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Quick Sight&lt;/a&gt; role ARN (needed later if you deploy the dashboard).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="stage-2-deploy-the-logging-stack-in-each-source-account"&gt;Stage 2: Deploy the logging stack in each source account&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deploy the linked account CloudFormation. Repeat this for every account running Amazon Bedrock workloads. The source stack is small and takes a single input: the destination ARN from Stage 1.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="stage-3-turn-on-amazon-bedrock-model-invocation-logging"&gt;Stage 3: Turn on Amazon Bedrock model invocation logging&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deploying the stack doesn’t start the logging. Amazon Bedrock logging is an account-level setting, not a CloudFormation resource. Activate it once per source account, pointing Amazon Bedrock at the log group and the logging role from Stage 2. Turn on CloudWatch delivery only.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="stage-4-verify-data-is-arriving"&gt;Stage 4: Verify data is arriving&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Run a test invocation in a source account, wait for Amazon Data Firehose to flush its buffer (&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/firehose/latest/dev/basic-deliver.html#frequency" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;roughly a minute, sometimes longer&lt;/a&gt;), then confirm Parquet objects have appeared under the data lake’s &lt;code&gt;data/&lt;/code&gt; prefix. Finally, check your S3 bucket to see data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="stage-5-create-the-athena-view-and-query-the-data"&gt;Stage 5: Create the Athena view and query the data&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The central stack saves the view definition as a named Athena query but doesn’t run it. Execute that saved query once to create the flattened view. Run queries in the dedicated Athena workgroup, against either the raw table or the flattened view. The view is straightforward: token counts, cache tokens, stop reason, and prompt/response previews are already flat columns.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-can-you-see-costs-of-your-amazon-bedrock-tokens"&gt;How can you see costs of your Amazon Bedrock tokens?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you want a dollar figure against all of this, join the processed table to your CUR 2.0 data. The join key is the IAM ARN. You can use the following example queries in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-token-cost-allocation/blob/main/Bedrock%20Data%20Lake%20SQL.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Bedrock Data Lake SQL&lt;/a&gt; file.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;That one join turns the observability lake into cost attribution too: which prompts cost the most, cost per team or account by day, and what your least efficient invocations are actually costing you. It’s a great addition, but the data lake earns its keep well before you get there.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-is-tokenomics-and-why-does-this-data-lake-matter-for-it"&gt;What is tokenomics and why does this data lake matter for it?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tokenomics, the practice of managing the economics of artificial intelligence (AI) token consumption, was announced as a formal FinOps domain at the 2026 FinOps Foundation conference. It’s built on the same See, Save, Run, and Plan pillars as Cloud Financial Management, but applied to tokens instead of infrastructure. The challenge is that you can’t optimize what you can’t measure. Most organizations today lack the granular data needed to answer basic tokenomics questions: which teams are consuming the most tokens, and which prompts are wasteful. With per-invocation token and cost visibility, teams can match each workload to the model that fits it. That’s exactly what this data lake gives you. With per-invocation visibility into prompts, responses, token counts, models, and IAM identities, you have the foundation to start running a tokenomics practice rather than only tracking an Amazon Bedrock bill.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="create-a-bedrock-invocation-log-dashboard"&gt;Create an Amazon Bedrock invocation log dashboard&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you want to visualize your token consumption, we have an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quicksight/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Quick Sight&lt;/a&gt; dashboard. This shows you the data from the table over time so you can share it with stakeholders in your organization. Using AWS Quick Sight, you can customize the dashboard and add organization metadata such as team information to make it your own. To deploy this, use the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-solutions-library-samples/cloud-intelligence-dashboards-framework/blob/main/CID-CMD.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;cid-cmd deploy&lt;/a&gt; CLI command. Full step-by-step deployment instructions are in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-token-cost-allocation" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;repository README&lt;/a&gt;, including AWS Quick Sight role configuration and datasource setup.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-21645-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-21645-2.png" alt="Amazon Quick Sight dashboard showing Bedrock token usage over time" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: AWS Quick Sight Amazon Bedrock dashboard&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-can-you-now-do-with-bedrock-invocation-logs"&gt;What can you now do with Amazon Bedrock invocation logs?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With every invocation in one fast, queryable table, you can finally answer questions that used to be a pain:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Debug and audit invocations –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Pull the exact prompt and response for specific calls, in an account, without digging through raw log files.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Measure token efficiency –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Compare &lt;code&gt;input_tokens&lt;/code&gt; against &lt;code&gt;output_tokens&lt;/code&gt; and model choice across teams to find where you’re burning tokens for no reason.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Spot prompt engineering opportunities –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Find requests where the response was cut off (&lt;code&gt;stop_reason = max_tokens&lt;/code&gt;), which is a good sign the prompt or config needs work.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Review model families –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The data shows which model serves each pattern so teams can confirm each model matches its workload.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Govern usage across teams –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Each invocation is tied to an account so you know who’s running what.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="data-governance"&gt;Data governance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because this solution centralizes full prompts and completions from every team into a single Amazon S3 bucket, security and governance are built into the architecture from the start. The bucket is protected by server-side encryption with AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS), so data is encrypted at rest by default. Access to the bucket and to Amazon Athena query results is controlled through AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) policies. You can scope permissions to the teams and roles that need them. Prompts might contain personally identifiable information (PII), depending on how your users interact with Amazon Bedrock. We recommend reviewing your IAM policies and Amazon S3 bucket policies to confirm that only authorized principals can reach the data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you wish to delete the resources made, empty the buckets of objects then delete the CloudFormation templates. Be mindful to empty the S3 buckets before deleting and delete the Athena Workgroup by hand.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="summary"&gt;Summary&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You now have a way to turn the raw invocation logs from Amazon Bedrock into a single, fast, org-wide observability layer. Instead of a closed system, you get a clear view of every prompt, response, token count, and model choice across your accounts. When you want it, you also get a direct line to the cost of each one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As with any system project, don’t try to boil the ocean. Start with one or two linked accounts, get comfortable querying the invocations table, then expand across the org. Your Amazon Bedrock usage is only going to grow, so the sooner you build this visibility, the more straightforward every future debugging, efficiency, and cost conversation gets.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you have any questions, or want to share how you’re building observability into your own workloads, visit the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-token-cost-allocation" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Sample Bedrock Invocation Data Lake GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;About the authors&lt;/h2&gt;

		<item>
		<title>How skills and agents work in AWS Quick</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-skills-and-agents-work-in-aws-quick/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Jed Lechner]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 10 Nov 2026 00:21:34 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[AWS Quick]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Best Practices]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">b0e4e95ccb178c5e51191d02022d9a631c23c383</guid>

					<description>Repeatable tasks like status updates, release notes, and ticket creation can be encoded in AWS Quick through agents and skills. This post covers how agents, skills, and your data fit together in AWS Quick, how to build and share skills for your team, and how the official catalog and custom agents help you use them.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;If you use AI agents, you’ve probably explained the same task more than once. You might describe how to build your weekly status update, write release notes, manage a project, or create tickets. These are all repeatable tasks that you can encode using &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Quick&lt;/a&gt; through agents and skills. AWS Quick is an AI-powered service for automating tasks, analyzing data, building web applications, and conducting research. You work with it in natural language, and Quick uses AI agents to process your requests against your connected data sources and applications.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we cover how agents, skills, and your data fit together in AWS Quick, and how to build and share skills that work for your team. We also show how the official catalog and custom agents help you put those skills to use.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="understanding-foundational-concepts"&gt;Understanding foundational concepts&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this section, we explain the foundational concepts that help you understand AWS Quick in relation to agents, skills, and your data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22266-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22266-1.png" alt="Hub-and-spoke diagram with the Quick agent at the center, connected to its instructions, skills, connectors, and data sources" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: How a Quick agent follows its instructions, loads the skills a task needs, acts through connectors, and grounds its answers in your data&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The agent sits at the center of Figure 1. It follows its instructions, loads the skills that a task calls for, takes action through connectors, and grounds its answers in your data: spaces, knowledge bases, datasets, and dashboards.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="working-with-agents"&gt;Working with agents&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/agents-desktop.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;agent&lt;/a&gt; in AWS Quick is a configurable AI with its own instructions, knowledge sources (spaces), and connectors, which it uses to answer questions and get work done for you. You can work with an agent in chat, or let it run on its own using a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/agents-desktop.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;routine&lt;/a&gt;. Agents can only reach the resources and services that you can, and you decide what each agent is allowed to do.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Quick&lt;/strong&gt; is the default built-in agent that every user has access to. It has no fixed scope, so in each conversation it works with whatever resources you have access to, such as your spaces, knowledge bases, dashboards, connectors, and skills. You don’t need to decide in advance which data or tools a request needs, so you can start most work with Quick.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A &lt;strong&gt;custom agent&lt;/strong&gt; is one that you or someone on your team configures for a specific job. An example is a human resources (HR) onboarding assistant that answers new-hire questions from the HR team’s space. You give it its own instructions and limit it to the spaces, connectors, and skills that your use case requires. After you test and publish it, you can share it with other users and groups within your AWS Quick account. For a walkthrough of building one, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-ai-powered-employee-onboarding-agents-with-amazon-quick/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Build AI-powered employee onboarding agents with AWS Quick&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="working-with-data"&gt;Working with data&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agents ground their answers in the data you connect to Quick. A &lt;strong&gt;space&lt;/strong&gt; is a curated collection of documents and AWS Quick resources that an agent uses as a source of information. You can create one for yourself, your team, or another group in your organization. You can upload files and folders to a space, or add assets such as dashboards, knowledge bases, action connectors, and datasets. When you start a conversation with Quick, you can attach a space to narrow the data that Quick uses for that conversation. When you add a space to an agent, the space becomes part of the agent’s configuration, and the agent draws on it when a request needs it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A &lt;strong&gt;knowledge base&lt;/strong&gt; is an organized, indexed collection of documents or content from data sources, optimized for retrieval and question answering. Quick syncs knowledge bases from sources such as Google Drive, Microsoft OneDrive, Confluence, Microsoft SharePoint, Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3), web crawler, or &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/knowledge-base.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Bases&lt;/a&gt;. Quick keeps them up to date on a sync schedule.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Datasets and dashboards come from &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/quick-bi.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Quick Sight&lt;/a&gt;, the business intelligence feature in Quick, which is available in accounts provisioned through the AWS Management Console. A &lt;strong&gt;dataset&lt;/strong&gt; is a prepared collection of data ready for use in analyses and dashboards. A &lt;strong&gt;dashboard&lt;/strong&gt; is the published version of an analysis that you share with other Quick users. With datasets, you can build semantically rich metadata over your structured data, such as database tables, so you can accomplish complex analytical use cases with natural language.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Lastly, a &lt;strong&gt;connector&lt;/strong&gt; links AWS Quick to an external application or a native tool, so agents can read data and take action on your behalf. &lt;strong&gt;Cloud connectors&lt;/strong&gt; connect Quick to services such as Microsoft Outlook, Slack, Jira, Asana, and Salesforce, and to remotely hosted Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers. They’re available on every device where you use Quick. &lt;strong&gt;Local connectors&lt;/strong&gt; run on your own machine, such as an MCP server that you host yourself, so their tools are available only when that machine is on and connected. For the full list of connector types, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/connections-desktop.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Connectors&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/integration-guides.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Integration-specific guides&lt;/a&gt; in the AWS Quick User Guide.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Together, these make your data, documents, and applications available through natural language, so you can ask questions and take actions in your connected sources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="working-with-skills"&gt;Working with skills&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/skills-desktop.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;skill&lt;/a&gt; is a reusable set of instructions that teaches an agent how to do a specific task. Skills are also the foundation of how an agent in Quick behaves and completes work. Like other agents, an agent in Quick runs in a harness (a managed agent loop) that follows the reasoning and acting (ReAct) pattern. When you supply AWS Quick with a prompt, the agent uses its available tools and instructions to accomplish its goal and supply you with a response. Agent frameworks such as the &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents SDK&lt;/a&gt; are built around the same model-driven loop.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Much of what Quick does by default comes from &lt;strong&gt;system skills&lt;/strong&gt;. For example, you can ask Quick to generate a Word document, a PowerPoint presentation, an Excel spreadsheet, or a PDF. A system skill can then create or edit the document using code with scripts the skill bundles. Other system skills browse the web, run code, execute deep research, transcribe audio and video, and generate images. Some manage Quick itself, such as building &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/using-amazon-quick-flows.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Quick Flows&lt;/a&gt;, scheduling routines, handing work to a coding agent such as Kiro, managing your memories and knowledge graph, or tracking your to-dos. Within Quick, you can turn system skills on or off, but you can’t edit, duplicate, export, publish, share, or delete them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Skills can do many kinds of work because of the capabilities and tools that AWS Quick supplies. For example, one skill can produce a deliverable, such as a weekly status update built from your template. Another can automate a workflow across your connectors, such as gathering the week’s Jira updates and posting a summary to Slack. A skill can also run calculations in Python scripts and chart the results with JavaScript.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-is-a-skill"&gt;What is a skill?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Quick skills follow the &lt;a href="https://agentskills.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Agent Skills&lt;/a&gt; specification, which Anthropic originally developed and released as an open standard. AWS Quick on desktop, which launched in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/04/amazon-quick-macos-windows-preview/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;preview in April 2026&lt;/a&gt; and became &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-quick-is-now-generally-available-on-desktop/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;generally available in September 2026&lt;/a&gt;, uses this specification.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A skill is a folder with a Markdown file &lt;code&gt;SKILL.md&lt;/code&gt; at its root, plus any optional scripts or other reference files its instructions use. The file’s frontmatter, the attributes between two &lt;code&gt;---&lt;/code&gt; lines, holds the name, description, and any other metadata relevant to your skill. See the following shortened example of a skill named &lt;code&gt;release-notes-generator&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;---
name: release-notes-generator
description: "Generates human-readable release notes from merged pull requests, commits, or changelogs ... Use when asked to 'generate release notes', 'write changelog', ..."
---&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The description and name are the two most important aspects to triggering a skill. Quick uses &lt;strong&gt;progressive disclosure&lt;/strong&gt; when it loads your skills into the agent’s prompt. The agent reads only each skill’s name and description until a request calls for that skill. It then loads the skill’s full instructions, as &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/skills-desktop.html#desktop-how-quick-loads-skills" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;How agents load skills&lt;/a&gt; describes. This means certain phrases can help trigger the skill, such as “generate my release notes for version 2.3.” The specification caps &lt;code&gt;name&lt;/code&gt; at 64 characters and &lt;code&gt;description&lt;/code&gt; at 1,024, and recommends instructions within the skill to be under 5,000 tokens.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22266-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22266-2.png" alt="Skill folder named release-notes-generator containing a SKILL.md file alongside optional scripts and references folders" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: The release-notes-generator skill folder, with a required SKILL.md file plus optional scripts and references folders&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="build-and-share-a-skill-in-quick"&gt;Build and share a skill in Quick&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can create a skill from chat, from a file (a .md or .zip file), or from a folder that has a &lt;code&gt;SKILL.md&lt;/code&gt; file at its root. This post focuses on chat. To start, describe your task to Quick in chat. For example, you can start with this template (replace the content between &amp;lt;&amp;gt; brackets with your requirements):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-auto"&gt;Every &amp;lt;specify your cadence e.g. every day, on monday, or none at all&amp;gt;, I have to grab data from the following sources:
- &amp;lt;List your sources and how you want to query them&amp;gt;
...

And do &amp;lt;describe how you want to transform the data and the exact sequence of steps&amp;gt;. 

Once I am finished, I have to create a report &amp;lt;describe your report format or provide an example&amp;gt; and send to the following stakeholders &amp;lt;describe your recipients&amp;gt; using the following connector &amp;lt;describe your connector&amp;gt;.

Create a skill from this requirement, and help me come up with testing scenarios to test the skill. &lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This is all you need to start building your own skills. Alternatively, you also can take a multi-turn conversation that you previously interacted with to accomplish a task and ask Quick to generate a skill from it. After you are satisfied with the final result, publish and share your skill:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Customize&lt;/strong&gt;, and then choose &lt;strong&gt;Skills&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Find your skill in draft, and open its detail panel.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Publish&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Share&lt;/strong&gt;, and then choose the people or groups to share it with.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Publishing and sharing are separate steps, so publishing alone doesn’t give anyone access to the skill. The people you share it with add it from &lt;strong&gt;Add to Quick&lt;/strong&gt; under &lt;strong&gt;Shared with me&lt;/strong&gt;, and their version stays in sync each time you publish again. For the current steps, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/skills-desktop.html#desktop-skill-draft-published" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Draft and published versions&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/skills-desktop.html#desktop-sharing-skills" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Sharing your skills&lt;/a&gt; in the AWS Quick User Guide.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="improve-your-skills-with-best-practices"&gt;Improve your skills with best practices&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A skill that you build from a quick description or conversation might work consistently for you, but there are many considerations you must take into account when building production skills that you share with your team. Skills are like code, and many engineering best practices can help any knowledge worker build them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;“It works on my machine” is a common phrase among software engineers. It describes software that runs for the person who built it but breaks when someone else installs or uses it. When building skills, you must consider your environment. Skill instructions can use agents to query data sources or invoke connectors or tools. Software engineers typically work around this limitation by documenting the prerequisites to using software. For example, if you are sharing a skill in isolation, you might want to create a README with your skill documenting these requirements. If you share an agent that uses your skill, also share the sources it connects to, or the people you share it with get answers only from the sources they can already access.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Plan for failures. Agents use connected sources that depend on third- and first-party services. When engineers write code, they need to understand what to do if a piece of software fails. If you incorporate these scenarios into your skill instructions, your agents can adapt based on concrete instructions. You don’t need to be an expert in the downstream service, because an agent works toward the goal and can retry or try another approach when a step fails. You do need to list what to do when a failure affects your task or business process. Take the following snippet from a workflow in a skill that might have ramifications if it fails.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;[Agent] Send the report to {{recipient}} over email.&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;What should the agent do if sending the email fails? You might want to add instructions on what to do in that scenario.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;[Agent] Send the report to {{recipient}} over email.
Validate: The report appears in your Sent folder.
If fails: Stop, and tell the user that the report wasn't sent and why, so they can send it themselves.&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Handling failure scenarios is even more important when executing agents on a schedule without a human in the loop.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Test every change. Software engineers write multiple forms of tests, such as unit and integration tests, that help them test software. These tests are run on every change. With skills, it is equally important that you generate your own test cases for various scenarios and continue to add new tests as you make changes. There are multiple ways to do this, but it’s best to test using the environment you expect your downstream users to use.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;There are many more aspects to consider when building production skills:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Add release notes to each change.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Keep hardcoded values that change, such as &lt;code&gt;/Users/me/notes&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;#my-team&lt;/code&gt;, out of the steps, and declare them as inputs that each person provides.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Record when the skill changed with human-readable dates.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Record where each reference file came from, so that if the source changes, you can update your skill instructions too.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Store a checksum, a fingerprint of the skill’s files that shows when a copy differs from the version that was checked.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Check the structure after every change, the way a linter catches mistakes before anyone reviews the code.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Build skills in a consistent way, so your team can contribute changes and review them faster.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For any user, this is a lot to consider. That’s why the &lt;a href="https://amazon-quick.github.io/Amazon-Quick-official-catalog/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Quick official catalog&lt;/a&gt; provides pre-built resources, such as skills, built with these best practices in mind.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="aws-quick-official-catalog"&gt;AWS Quick official catalog&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The catalog is a collection of agent resources that you can install and use as they are, or take as a starting point and adapt to your own work. The catalog states that every resource in it is tested and scanned for quality, security, and other factors before it’s published. The skills cover everyday productivity and the work of teams such as sales, marketing, HR, legal, project management, customer success, and software engineering. They also cover industries such as finance, healthcare and life sciences, energy and utilities, hospitality, and supply chain and logistics.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The catalog includes the &lt;strong&gt;Skill Architect&lt;/strong&gt; skill, a pre-built skill that applies these engineering best practices for you, as &lt;a href="https://amazon-quick.github.io/Amazon-Quick-official-catalog/build-skills/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Build skills with software engineering practices in AWS Quick&lt;/a&gt; describes. To install it, choose &lt;strong&gt;Customize&lt;/strong&gt;, &lt;strong&gt;Skills&lt;/strong&gt;, &lt;strong&gt;Browse all&lt;/strong&gt;, and then &lt;strong&gt;Official&lt;/strong&gt;. Enter &lt;code&gt;skill architect&lt;/code&gt; in the search box, and then choose &lt;strong&gt;Install&lt;/strong&gt;. For the current steps, see &lt;a href="https://amazon-quick.github.io/Amazon-Quick-official-catalog/install/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Install skills in AWS Quick on desktop&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="connect-skills-to-your-agents"&gt;Connect skills to your agents&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you create an agent, you configure its name, description, and instructions, and you choose the spaces it reads from, the connectors it can use, and the skills it can load. For details, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/agents-desktop.html#desktop-agent-configuration" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Agent configuration&lt;/a&gt; in the AWS Quick User Guide. An agent works best when it solves a particular use case, such as answering new-hire questions or preparing a weekly status report, and &lt;strong&gt;bounding&lt;/strong&gt; its world to the spaces, skills, and connectors that use case requires. By default, an agent can use all of your skills, and when you add specific skills, the agent is limited to those. That becomes problematic when you share the agent, because it runs with the access of the person using it. The same request can reach different data and skills for each person, and can return incomplete answers when it needs a source that only you can access.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you want to tie them all (agents, skills, and spaces) together, try out the &lt;a href="https://catalog.us-east-1.prod.workshops.aws/workshops/a8484e57-2e30-40ee-bd98-0122f0d05acc/en-US/2000-build-your-personal-setup/2100-run-personal-agent-architect" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;personal agent architect module&lt;/a&gt; in the linked workshop.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed how agents use skills and your data in AWS Quick, and how to build and share skills that work for your team. We also showed how the official catalog and custom agents help you put those skills to use.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more, find events, or share your success with AWS Quick, visit the &lt;a href="https://community.amazonquick.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Quick Community&lt;/a&gt;. The AWS Quick Community has learning material, feature announcements, events, and examples from other users. You can also ask questions in its Q&amp;amp;A forum.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For a recorded session on the topics in this post, watch &lt;a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=m9gN2GapOps" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Encode Your Enterprise with Agents &amp;amp; Skills on AWS Quick&lt;/a&gt;. The session covers how a skill is structured and loaded, how skills work with agents, spaces, and connectors, and best practices for building skills that you share. It ends with a demo of the official catalog and the Skill Architect skill.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Here are some additional resources designed by the specialists who work on AWS Quick every day:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://catalog.workshops.aws/amazon-quick-suite-workshop" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;A Complete Guide to AWS Quick: The Agentic AI Business Workspace&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://catalog.us-east-1.prod.workshops.aws/workshops/8724e6e3-1115-4f40-9770-8a24199ce308/en-US" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Quick: End-to-End Business Use Cases with Rich Integrations&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://catalog.us-east-1.prod.workshops.aws/workshops/767bc5b8-1d33-40e2-8969-f8653db64756/en-US" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Quick Administration Workshop&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://catalog.workshops.aws/quicksight/en-US" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Deep dive into AWS Quick Sight&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws-samples.github.io/sample-amazon-quick-suite-knowledge-hub/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Quick Knowledge Hub – Official Code Samples and Solutions&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/search/?searchQuery=Amazon%20Quick&amp;amp;filters=((id:provider,value:(blogs)))&amp;amp;sort=(id:relevance,direction:desc)" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS blog posts about AWS Quick&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt;
