			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Taxonomy of agentic primitives in AWS Quick</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/taxonomy-of-agentic-primitives-in-amazon-quick/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Thomas Delteil]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 06 Nov 2026 17:34:45 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[AWS Quick]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Thought Leadership]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">755c7d8248913b9c1698eceff615a3e12637e5e9</guid>

					<description>As we built Amazon Quick, its agents grew from answering questions to taking actions, and the same primitives kept getting rebuilt under different names. This post shares the taxonomy we converged on — triggers, agents, capabilities, and learned context — found by decomposing agentic work into four plain questions: When, What, Who, and How.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Over the past year, as we built AWS Quick, our agents went from answering questions to taking actions: scheduling meetings, running research, generating reports. As the system evolved, one problem kept resurfacing: there was no shared definition of the primitives that agentic work is built from. The same few ideas kept getting rebuilt under different names: an actor that does the work, a signal that starts it, a packaged procedure, and a memory that carries lessons forward. This repetition leaked into product design and onto the user.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Take the user who says “&lt;em&gt;monitor my Slack channel and send me a daily digest&lt;/em&gt;”. Is that a new agent, a scheduled instruction to an existing one, a workflow, or a skill? These are different questions, but without a shared vocabulary they collapse into one confusing choice.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post shares the taxonomy we converged on: one set of primitives found by decomposing agentic work into four plain questions: &lt;em&gt;When?&lt;/em&gt;, &lt;em&gt;What?&lt;/em&gt;, &lt;em&gt;Who?&lt;/em&gt;, and &lt;em&gt;How?&lt;/em&gt; Most of the framework stays invisible to end users by design, but a shared vocabulary makes the system easier to reason about for anyone building beneath it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-framework-at-a-glance"&gt;The framework at a glance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/03/ML-21505-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/03/ML-21505-1.png" alt="Triggers (When and What) feed an agent (Who) that uses capabilities (How) along a less-to-more deterministic axis, with a session runtime and background learning" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: How triggers, agents, and capabilities relate, with learning feeding back from the session runtime&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A &lt;strong&gt;trigger&lt;/strong&gt; starts the work and carries the task (&lt;em&gt;When&lt;/em&gt; and &lt;em&gt;What&lt;/em&gt;), an &lt;strong&gt;agent&lt;/strong&gt; takes it on (&lt;em&gt;Who&lt;/em&gt;) and uses its &lt;strong&gt;capabilities&lt;/strong&gt; (tools, skills, and workflows) to perform the task (&lt;em&gt;How&lt;/em&gt;). This post describes these primitives and how they relate to one another. It’s the first post in a series, and upcoming posts cover our agentic operating system, background learning, proactive feeds, and much more.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="triggers-what-causes-work-to-begin"&gt;Triggers: What causes work to begin&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Triggers are the activation mechanism for getting work done, but they also carry instructions: a human message is both the moment of activation and the description of what to do. They are both the &lt;em&gt;When?&lt;/em&gt; and the &lt;em&gt;What?&lt;/em&gt; of a task.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Human-initiated:&lt;/strong&gt; A person opens a conversation and asks the agent to do something. Interactive, synchronous, conversational.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agent-delegated:&lt;/strong&gt; One agent, mid-task, invokes another to handle a sub-problem. Programmatic, composable, potentially recursive.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scheduled:&lt;/strong&gt; Time-based activation, like “run financial research every Monday at 9am.” Deterministic, with the same standing instructions applied across runs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Event-driven:&lt;/strong&gt; A signal from an external system, such as a new email, a Slack mention, a file change, or a webhook.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The type of trigger conditions the execution: a scheduled background task might stay conservative (for example, do nothing if uncertain), while a human-initiated one would be responsive (for example, ask rather than stop).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="agents"&gt;Agents&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We define an agent as a &lt;em&gt;persistent, learning actor&lt;/em&gt;. It’s the &lt;em&gt;Who?&lt;/em&gt; in any execution, and the anchor against which learning accrues. Two agents given identical input can differ, because they carry different instructions, capabilities, and histories. The agent isn’t the instructions it follows. It’s the entity that interprets them. It consists of three things:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Instructions:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Standing guidance defining persona, role, context files, and operating principles. A Finance Agent behaves differently from a general assistant primarily because of this.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Capabilities:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The tools, skills, workflows, and sub-agents available for use, updated dynamically. A charting library can be added in one session and removed the next.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Learned context:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Memory that accumulates through use: the queries that work for a tool, the formatting users prefer, the edge cases they care about. This is how the tenth execution beats the first without anyone changing the instructions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Everything else is extrinsic: the large language model (LLM) underlying the agent is a swappable model parameter, conversation history is per-session state, and triggers belong to the platform.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A consequence of this definition is that a user request “&lt;em&gt;I want an agent that monitors my Slack and sends a digest every morning&lt;/em&gt;” is usually a &lt;em&gt;trigger added to an existing agent&lt;/em&gt;, not a new agent or workflow. With this framing, we can merge chat, scheduled, and “autonomous” agents into one actor that you can converse with, schedule, and that can orchestrate and inspect its own past work.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="capabilities"&gt;Capabilities&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Capabilities are &lt;em&gt;How&lt;/em&gt; an agent acts, spanning a spectrum from fully agentic to fully deterministic. That axis is really a &lt;em&gt;confidence contract&lt;/em&gt;: how much latitude the agent keeps versus how much the user fixes in advance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tools&lt;/strong&gt; are atomic operations with a typed schema: send an email, query a database, read a file. Whether a local function, a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server, or another agent behind a protocol boundary, the caller sees the same interface: name, description, parameters, result.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Skills&lt;/strong&gt; are structured guidance with room for judgment: instructions bundled with resources (templates, scripts, examples) that describe an approach rather than a rigid sequence. They load through &lt;em&gt;progressive disclosure&lt;/em&gt;, so the agent sees only names and short descriptions until one becomes relevant, then reads the full instructions in. A skill can therefore bundle arbitrary depth of expertise without a constant cost.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Workflows&lt;/strong&gt; are deterministic coordination: declared steps, control flow, branching, and error handling that execute in fixed order, with decision logic in code rather than model inference. Think of a purchase-approval route: budget check, manager sign-off, then finance, each recorded. The route is fixed, but the judgment &lt;em&gt;inside&lt;/em&gt; a step is still the agent’s.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/03/ML-21505-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/03/ML-21505-2.png" alt="A skill lets the agent interpret guidance and choose the order (less deterministic); a workflow fixes the step order in code (more deterministic)" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Skills versus workflows, which differ in whether the agent or the code decides the step order&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-each-primitive-earns-its-keep"&gt;Why skills and workflows outlast model improvements&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A natural question arises: if models keep improving, won’t skills and workflows become unnecessary? We don’t think so, because each holds value independent of model capability. Skills are the unit for capturing, versioning, and sharing institutional expertise, while workflows deliver auditability, step-wise visibility, and deterministic multi-service coordination. Nor are they fixed: a well-understood skill can tighten into a workflow (&lt;em&gt;crystallization&lt;/em&gt;), a workflow that keeps hitting edge cases can loosen back into a skill (&lt;em&gt;dissolution&lt;/em&gt;). This is done in Quick through our continuous learning framework, which we will describe in a future post.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-this-means-in-practice"&gt;What this means in practice&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Return to “&lt;em&gt;monitor my Slack channel and send me a daily digest&lt;/em&gt;”. Because these primitives compose, the same request can be met several valid ways:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A scheduled task with a one-line instruction, leaving the agent to figure out the rest.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The same task backed by a skill with explicit formatting rules and channel priorities.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The same task invoking a workflow with deterministic steps and error handling.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;All three produce a daily digest on a schedule. What differs is the confidence contract. A user who trusts the agent wants the first, one burned by inconsistency the second, one running a compliance-sensitive process the third.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Learning shifts that trajectory over time. A task that starts as one sentence (“&lt;em&gt;check my email for urgent items&lt;/em&gt;”) accumulates domain-bound learnings: effective queries, preferred formatting, and which senders matter. The tenth run is markedly better, with no one encoding that by hand. The right design is the simplest option that meets the user’s confidence requirement, with a path to graduate through autonomous learning when they need more.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The value of this agentic primitive taxonomy is that four plain questions, &lt;em&gt;When&lt;/em&gt;, &lt;em&gt;What&lt;/em&gt;, &lt;em&gt;Who&lt;/em&gt;, and &lt;em&gt;How&lt;/em&gt;, are enough to place any piece of agentic work, from a one-line chat request to a scheduled, compliance-grade process. Sharing one vocabulary lets builders reason about the system and lets the product hide complexity. Best of all, the user never has to learn any of it. They say what they want, and the system assembles the appropriate primitives to do their job.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Next in the series: the agentic operating system that builds on these primitives and powers AWS Quick.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="keep-exploring"&gt;Keep exploring&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Ready to try? Start with the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Quick getting-started guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;About the authors&lt;/h2&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Serverless MCP server for AI agent data lake access on Amazon ECS Fargate</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/serverless-mcp-server-for-ai-agent-data-lake-access-on-amazon-ecs-fargate/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Aishwarya Pawar]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 06 Nov 2026 17:13:13 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Elastic Container Service]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AWS Fargate]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">64b64349a40669287b5c781082e75c92e2cc9153</guid>

					<description>Learn how to build a serverless Model Context Protocol (MCP) server on Amazon ECS with AWS Fargate that gives AI agents secure, metadata-driven access to an enterprise data lake. Exposing a new dataset requires only a JSON file, with no per-dataset integration code.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Organizations building applications powered by artificial intelligence (AI) face a recurring challenge: giving large language model (LLM) agents access to enterprise data lakes without duplicating integration logic across every agent and application. Most teams begin by wiring agents directly to databases and APIs, with no shared gateway such as a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server in between. Each new dataset demands custom code, and each agent reimplements its own access control. As the number of datasets or agents grows, permission enforcement drifts, and onboarding a new data source becomes a multi-day engineering effort.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Our team supports Amazon’s North America Sort Center (NASC) network, where planning teams rely on operational data to manage capacity, labor, and package flow. To give those teams conversational access to that data, we built a serverless &lt;a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model Context Protocol (MCP)&lt;/a&gt; server on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fargate/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS)&lt;/a&gt; with AWS Fargate to address this problem for planning teams in NASC. The server acts as a centralized, agent-agnostic gateway between AI agents and our enterprise data lake. Exposing a new dataset requires only a JSON metadata file that the server discovers automatically on the next pipeline sync.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we describe the architecture, show how a metadata-driven approach removes the need for per-dataset integration code, and explain how the serverless infrastructure supports multiple agents and applications in a multi-tenant environment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="problem-statement-and-design-goals"&gt;Problem statement and design goals&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two interrelated challenges shaped how we designed this system: fragmented data access patterns and an unsustainable onboarding model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="integration-challenge-at-scale"&gt;Integration challenge at scale&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Our data lake contains thousands of datasets spanning operational metrics, business analytics, and domain-specific data. Multiple teams wanted AI assistants that could query this data conversationally, but each built its own integration from scratch. Each agent embedded its own SQL generation logic, schema knowledge, and connection handling, so a schema change meant patching every agent individually. Access control was fragmented, with no single audit point for data access decisions. Onboarding a new dataset required custom tool code, query validation, and redeployment, typically a multi-day effort per table.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="design-goals"&gt;Design goals&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We established four requirements:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configuration-driven data lake access. Adding a dataset requires only a metadata file, not code.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Agent-agnostic tool interfaces. Any MCP-compatible client can invoke the same tools.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Centralized permission enforcement. Access control is enforced at the MCP server layer.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Elastic infrastructure. The system scales with bursty AI workload patterns.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture separates concerns into four layers (client, MCP server, tools, and knowledge-base refresh), each independently scalable and deployable. A metadata catalog underpins the tools layer, storing schema descriptions, column-level semantics, and table relationships that the AI agent references when generating SQL. Because the agent derives schema knowledge from the catalog at query time, there is no need to hardcode table structures or redeploy when schemas change. The knowledge-base refresh layer keeps that catalog current, indexing new dataset metadata as it arrives. The following sections describe each layer in detail.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architecture-walkthrough"&gt;Architecture walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the request flow from user query to data layer across all four layers. A user submits a query from a client application (web chat, Slack, an IDE plugin, or an external agent). The request resolves through Amazon Route 53 for DNS and reaches an Application Load Balancer in a public subnet, configured with HTTPS and AWS WAF. The load balancer routes to Amazon Cognito, which authenticates the user and enriches the request. Authenticated traffic then enters the private subnet, where the client layer runs on Amazon ECS Fargate. This layer handles session state in Amazon DynamoDB and forwards requests through an internal Application Load Balancer to the MCP server layer, also on Amazon ECS Fargate. In the MCP server layer, an AI agent powered by Amazon Bedrock orchestrates tools built with FastMCP (a Python framework for building MCP servers). Amazon Bedrock Guardrails is applied to every model interaction. On the right, the data layer holds the tool backends. Amazon Athena and Amazon Redshift run SQL queries against the data lake, and Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases, the fully managed Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) capability, handles semantic search. Data ingestion into Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) and the knowledge bases is handled by AWS Lambda. Each layer is described in detail in the sections that follow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-20343-1.jpeg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-20343-1.jpeg" alt="Architecture diagram of the serverless MCP server showing request flow from client applications through Route 53, AWS WAF, an Application Load Balancer, and Amazon Cognito into the client and MCP server layers on Amazon ECS Fargate, then to the data layer." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Request flow through the serverless MCP server, from client applications to the data layer&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="mcp-client-layer-authentication-and-session-management"&gt;MCP client layer: Authentication and session management&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A request flows from the client application through Amazon Route 53 for DNS resolution to an Application Load Balancer configured with HTTPS and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/waf/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS WAF&lt;/a&gt;. The load balancer forwards traffic to the Amazon ECS Fargate client service, which integrates with Amazon Cognito for JSON Web Token (JWT) authentication and Amazon DynamoDB for session storage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A context mapping configuration drives this enrichment, scoping each query to only the relevant tables. In our testing, this scoping keeps end-to-end interaction latency in the 15–20 second range. The following example shows a context mapping configuration:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "package_data": {
    "description": "Package tracking and delivery information",
    "tables": ["analytics_db.regional_order_summary", "operations_gold.load_summary"],
    "sources": {"wiki": [], "quip": []}
  },
  "labor_planning": {
    "description": "Workforce management and capacity planning",
    "tables": ["workforce.capacity_details"],
    "sources": {"wiki": [], "quip": []}
  }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a context specifies tables, the MCP server narrows its search of the metadata catalog to those tables, reducing noise and improving SQL generation accuracy. Adding a new business domain requires only adding an entry to this mapping.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="mcp-server-layer-stateless-execution-with-conversation-state"&gt;MCP server layer: Stateless execution with conversation state&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The MCP server runs on its own ECS Fargate cluster, isolated from the client layer. Requests reach it through Amazon Route 53 and an internal Application Load Balancer in a private subnet. The server handles full orchestration including query analysis, tool selection, execution, and response generation. Through the MCP protocol endpoint (&lt;code&gt;/mcp&lt;/code&gt;), a FastMCP-powered JSON-RPC interface, external MCP-compatible clients such as Amazon Q Developer or IDE extensions can discover and invoke tools directly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Fargate tasks are stateless with conversation state persisted in DynamoDB, so tasks can be replaced or scaled without data loss. When a request arrives, the agent retrieves session history, analyzes user intent using Amazon Bedrock, discovers and invokes appropriate tools, and streams results back to the user.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="tools-layer-and-metadata-driven-data-lake-access"&gt;Tools layer and metadata-driven data lake access&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each tool is registered with FastMCP and discoverable at runtime:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Sr.no&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Backend&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Purpose&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;SQL query tools&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; &lt;p&gt;Amazon Athena&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;Amazon Redshift&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;SQL queries against any configured dataset in the data lake and Redshift&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Document and metadata search&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Semantic search over internal documentation, table metadata and team wikis&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Policy search&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Search operational policies and standard procedures&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Athena query tools are the primary interface to the data lake. The agent first queries the metadata catalog to understand available schemas, generates an optimized SQL query through Amazon Bedrock, validates and auto-corrects the SQL for Athena compatibility, then executes it. Results flow back to the orchestrating agent for natural language synthesis.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="responsible-ai-controls"&gt;Responsible AI controls&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails is applied to all LLM interactions and is designed to filter harmful content, enforce grounding checks, and help block prompt injection attempts. These guardrails operate as production controls at the MCP server layer, so every connected agent benefits from the same content safety policies regardless of how it connects. The Athena query tools enforce &lt;code&gt;SELECT&lt;/code&gt;-only queries, rejecting write operations at the tool level before they reach the data lake.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="knowledge-base-refresh-layer-dataset-onboarding-with-a-single-json-file"&gt;Knowledge base refresh layer: Dataset onboarding with a single JSON file&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The entire system is built around configuration-driven extensibility. Adding a new dataset requires a metadata JSON file describing the table and an optional context mapping entry. The file captures the table’s schema, business context, and column-level semantics. It serves as the single source of truth that the AI agent uses to understand and query the dataset:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "database_service_name": "athena",
  "database_name": "analytics_db",
  "table_name": "regional_order_summary",
  "business_description_for_the_table": "Regional order history with shipping status and delivery performance. Contains completed orders with delivery data. Refreshes daily. In Athena SQL, always use date() to wrap string literals when comparing against timestamp columns.",
  "column_details": {
    "order_id [varchar]": {
      "description": "Unique order identifier assigned at checkout.",
      "sample_values": ["ORD-20250115-001234", "ORD-20250116-005678"]
    },
    "order_date [timestamp(3)]": {
      "description": "Date and time the order was placed. Use this column for time-range filtering.",
      "sample_values": ["2025-01-15 08:00:00.000", "2025-01-16 12:30:00.000"]
    },
    "warehouse_id [varchar]": {
      "description": "Fulfillment center that processed the order.",
      "sample_values": ["FC-EAST-07", "FC-WEST-03", "FC-SOUTH-12"]
    }
  },
  "load_type": "Incremental (refreshes last 7 days)",
  "refresh_frequency": "Daily at 6:00 AM UTC",
  "table_size": "~2.1B rows",
  "creation_time": "2024-03-15",
  "last_refresh_time": "2025-05-20T06:00:00Z"
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;business_description_for_the_table&lt;/code&gt; field carries engine-specific query hints, such as “always use &lt;code&gt;date()&lt;/code&gt; to wrap string literals,” that reduce SQL validation failures. The &lt;code&gt;column_details&lt;/code&gt; field pairs each &lt;code&gt;column_name [type]&lt;/code&gt; with a natural language description and sample values, giving the agent both schema awareness and business semantics. The &lt;code&gt;sample_values&lt;/code&gt; field matters for accurate &lt;code&gt;WHERE&lt;/code&gt; clause generation because the agent learns real data patterns, for instance that &lt;code&gt;warehouse_id&lt;/code&gt; holds codes like &lt;code&gt;FC-EAST-07&lt;/code&gt; rather than spelled-out names like “East region facility.”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The pipeline picks up metadata files, generates vector embeddings with Amazon Titan Text Embeddings, and indexes them into Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases. Once indexed, the table is discoverable by all connected agents without manual re-indexing or server restarts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To improve accuracy for specific applications, an engineer can optionally associate the dataset with a business context. This narrows the search scope, so the agent prioritizes the right tables. The following example shows a context mapping entry:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "delivery_metrics": {
    "description": "Delivery accuracy and defect attribution data",
    "tables": ["analytics_db.customer_orders_summary"],
    "sources": {"wiki": [], "quip": []}
  }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If no context is specified, the agent searches across all indexed tables. The context mapping reduces latency and improves SQL accuracy by limiting the search space. This scoping is optional and can be added at any time without modifying the metadata files themselves.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="putting-it-together-a-query-in-action"&gt;Putting it together: A query in action&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a user asks, “What were our top 10 highest-value orders last month?”, the client enriches the request with business context to narrow scope. The agent converts the question into a vector embedding, searches the metadata catalog for matching schemas, and selects the most relevant table via a lightweight LLM call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Using the selected schema, column descriptions, and engine-specific query hints, the agent generates a SQL statement such as:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;SELECT order_id, customer_id, total_amount, order_date
FROM analytics_db.regional_order_summary
WHERE order_date &amp;gt;= date_add('month', -1, current_date)
ORDER BY total_amount DESC
LIMIT 10&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The SQL is validated and auto-corrected for engine compatibility, then executed against Amazon Athena. The agent formats the results into a natural language response and streams it back to the user. In our testing, this end-to-end flow completes within 15–20 seconds.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same orchestration extends beyond pure SQL. Because document search is registered as another tool on the same server, the agent can answer questions that span structured and unstructured sources in a single turn, such as “What was our return rate for Q4, and what does the product team’s analysis say about the root causes?” For that request, the agent runs an Athena query for the return-rate figure and a Knowledge Bases document search for the analysis, then combines both into one response.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="security-and-multi-tenant-access"&gt;Security and multi-tenant access&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;That single-turn orchestration works only because every tool call passes through the same controlled gateway. The architecture uses layered security rather than a single enforcement point. The MCP server runs in private subnets behind an internal Application Load Balancer and is not reachable from the internet. Only the client layer, which sits behind a public-facing Application Load Balancer with AWS WAF, can route requests to it. Amazon Cognito validates user identity at the client layer, and only authenticated, token-bearing requests are forwarded downstream. At the tool level, the Athena query tools enforce &lt;code&gt;SELECT&lt;/code&gt;-only queries, rejecting write operations before they reach the data lake. All tool invocations are logged to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt; with structured JSON, which gives security teams a single audit point for data access across every agent and application. This architecture is available in the AWS Regions where Amazon Bedrock and Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases are available.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This centralized design is also what makes the server multi-tenant. Because the standardized &lt;code&gt;/mcp&lt;/code&gt; endpoint accepts any MCP-compatible client regardless of which LLM or orchestration framework it uses, multiple agents share the same tools and the same access controls. Adding a new agent requires only pointing it at the server endpoint, so onboarding another team extends the system without weakening or duplicating the security model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="serverless-infrastructure-and-scaling"&gt;Serverless infrastructure and scaling&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each layer scales through a mechanism suited to its workload pattern:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Layer&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scaling mechanism&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Trigger&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Client (ECS Fargate)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ECS Service Auto Scaling&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CPU utilization (50% threshold)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;MCP Server (ECS Fargate)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ECS Service Auto Scaling&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CPU/Memory utilization&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Athena&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Serverless (automatic)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Query concurrency&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Managed service scaling&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Inference requests&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon ECS Fargate provides serverless compute with no EC2 instances to manage. Downstream services (Athena, Bedrock) scale automatically, so the system handles a single query or hundreds of concurrent queries without configuration changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="use-cases-and-extensions"&gt;Use cases and extensions&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This architecture supports several patterns beyond basic question-answering. Enterprise knowledge assistants can combine data lake queries with document search to answer complex questions spanning both structured data and unstructured knowledge, such as “What’s our return rate for Q4, and what does the product team’s analysis say about the root causes?” Internal analytics copilots let business users query the data lake conversationally without writing SQL, relying on the metadata descriptions and query hints to guide the agent toward accurate, performant query patterns.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The standardized &lt;code&gt;/mcp&lt;/code&gt; endpoint also supports multi-agent workflows. Multiple specialized agents for data analysis, document retrieval, and operational alerting can share the same MCP server, each accessing the full tool suite through a common protocol. Onboarding a new team’s data follows the same configuration-driven pattern. They add a metadata JSON file, and within one pipeline sync cycle their dataset is discoverable by every connected agent and application.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A centralized MCP server on Amazon ECS Fargate provides a scalable, secure gateway between AI agents and enterprise data lakes. The metadata-driven approach removes the need for per-dataset integration code, and the Model Context Protocol standard means any MCP-compatible client can connect without custom adapters.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The key architectural decision is separating the tool layer from the agent layer. Agents evolve rapidly, but data access patterns should outlive any single agent implementation. Treating extensibility as a configuration problem rather than a code problem accelerates onboarding.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fargate/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon ECS on AWS Fargate&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;, the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/knowledge-base.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/a&gt; user guide, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/athena/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Athena&lt;/a&gt;, and the &lt;a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/2026-07-28/getting-started/intro" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model Context Protocol specification&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="next-steps"&gt;Next steps&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We are extending this architecture in two directions. First, finer-grained access control where table owners can mark sensitive columns as restricted per permission group, and the agent automatically excludes those columns from generated SQL without prompt-level changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Second, closing the feedback loop so that when queries fail or produce low-confidence results, the system captures the failure pattern and surfaces targeted suggestions for improving column descriptions, adding query hints.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;About the authors&lt;/h2&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Govern AI agent access with Okta Cross App Access and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore – Part 2</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/govern-ai-agent-access-with-okta-cross-app-access-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-part-2/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Hammad Ausaf]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 06 Nov 2026 17:09:23 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Expert (400)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">568f99493ce9fc7f0fbe42c57947610f9ca4666c</guid>

					<description>In Part 1 you built one governed agent. Part 2 scales the pattern to an enterprise: Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway becomes the single front door and redeems Okta's grant, Cedar policies bind each tool to the agent, a second agent runs on Amazon EKS, and AgentCore Observability traces every allow and deny beside Okta's log in Amazon CloudWatch.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;In &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/govern-ai-agent-access-with-okta-cross-app-access-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-part-1/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Part 1&lt;/a&gt;, you built a task agent on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;, a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. The agent runs on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, and acts for signed-in users through Okta Cross App Access (XAA). Okta decided which agent might act for which user, and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, redeemed Okta’s grant inside the agent. That proves the governance mechanism, and it runs on a deliberately small topology: one agent, two Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers, and the authorization check written into each server.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The mechanism scales. The topology doesn’t. Three things that are fine for one agent and two tools become the work in an enterprise with hundreds of agents and thousands of tools:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What Part 1 does&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Why it stops scaling&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What Part 2 does instead&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The agent holds each MCP server’s runtime ARN and calls it directly&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;N agents against M servers is a wiring matrix, and every new tool touches every agent that needs it&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agents reach one Gateway endpoint, and the Gateway holds the target registry&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The agent’s role holds the token-exchange permission and reads the provider’s secret&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Every agent that acts for users gets a credential path to every resource it might reach&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The Gateway redeems the grant, and the agent’s role holds no token-exchange or secret permissions&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Each MCP server carries the same &lt;code&gt;groups&lt;/code&gt; check in its own code&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The same rule, copied into thousands of servers, drifts and can’t be audited or changed in one place&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;One default-deny Cedar policy set, evaluated at the Gateway before tools run&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you make that change on the Part 1 solution. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, becomes the single front door and redeems Okta’s grant for each target. Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore evaluates Cedar policies that bind each tool to the agent and the scope Okta delegated. A second agent on Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS) joins under its own Okta identity, to show that the front door doesn’t care where an agent runs. And Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Observability, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, with Amazon CloudWatch gives you one place to read every decision, including Okta’s. We also compare XAA with per-user OAuth consent for applications that can’t accept Okta’s grant.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-changes-from-part-1"&gt;What changes from Part 1&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Okta configuration, the AgentCore Identity credential provider, and the MCP servers from Part 1 stay as they are. Figure 1 shows the architecture with the additions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-1.png" alt="Part 1 solution extended with AgentCore Gateway, Policy, and Observability, a second agent on Amazon EKS, and the audit path" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: The Part 1 solution with AgentCore Gateway, Policy, and Observability, a second agent on Amazon EKS, and the audit path, with numbers matching the steps that follow&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The user signs in, and the backend for frontend (BFF) exchanges the ID token for an Identity Assertion JWT Authorization Grant (ID-JAG) issued to the task agent, agent A, as in Part 1.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The BFF invokes agent A on AgentCore runtime with the ID-JAG.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Agent A forwards the ID-JAG to its Gateway as the bearer token of its MCP session. The Gateway’s inbound authorizer accepts only agent A’s ID-JAGs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Policy in AgentCore evaluates Cedar policies for each tool listing and each tool call, before the Gateway invokes a target.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For calls that Cedar permits, the Gateway has AgentCore Identity redeem the ID-JAG for each target at Okta’s custom authorization server through the Part 1 credential provider. A call that Cedar denies doesn’t redeem the ID-JAG.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The MCP server checks the user’s groups and scope, as in Part 1, and records each decision as an &lt;code&gt;authz.check&lt;/code&gt; span.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Every hop emits OpenTelemetry spans to AgentCore Observability, which CloudWatch Transaction Search collects, and Okta streams its System Log to CloudWatch through Amazon EventBridge.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The reporting agent, agent B, runs on Amazon EKS and follows the same path through its own Gateway.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In addition to the Part 1 solution, you need the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prerequisite&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Needed for&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;eksctl, kubectl, and Docker&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The second agent on Amazon EKS&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Okta Log Streaming&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Okta’s System Log in CloudWatch&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) permissions&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AgentCore Gateways and policy engines, Amazon EKS clusters, CloudWatch log groups, and Amazon EventBridge rules&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The additions take about 90 minutes. The EKS control plane costs $0.10 per hour, plus nodes and the Amazon EKS Auto Mode management fee, and the AgentCore features charge for what you use. See &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eks/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon EKS pricing&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore pricing&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As in Part 1, the base for this solution is the AWS sample &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/05-authenticate-and-authorize/08-okta-xaa-delegation-chain" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;08-okta-xaa-delegation-chain&lt;/a&gt; on GitHub, and you can build the Part 2 additions on top of it. Beyond that sample, the snippets and configurations in this post show the pattern conceptually. They aren’t a complete, deployable solution. The code is sample code for non-production use, so work with your security and legal teams to meet your organization’s security, regulatory, and compliance requirements before you deploy it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="create-a-gateway-that-accepts-the-id-jag"&gt;Create a Gateway that accepts the ID-JAG&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Gateway’s inbound authorizer works like the runtime authorizer from Part 1. Its issuer is Okta’s org authorization server, its audience is the custom authorization server, and &lt;code&gt;allowedClients&lt;/code&gt; pins it to one agent:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;authorizer = {"customJWTAuthorizer": {
    "discoveryUrl": f"{OKTA_ORG}/.well-known/openid-configuration",  # the ID-JAG's issuer
    "allowedAudience": [f"{OKTA_ORG}/oauth2/{CUSTOM_AS_ID}"],         # the resource's authorization server
    "allowedClients": [AGENT_CLIENT_ID],                               # only this agent's ID-JAGs
    "allowedScopes": ["xaa:read", "xaa:write"],
}}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 2 shows the resulting inbound authorizer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-2.png" alt="Gateway inbound authorizer accepting only ID-JAGs that Okta issued to the agent for the resource’s authorization server" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: The Gateway accepts only ID-JAGs that Okta issued to the agent for the resource’s authorization server&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each MCP server becomes a Gateway target whose outbound authorization uses &lt;code&gt;TOKEN_EXCHANGE&lt;/code&gt; through the Part 1 credential provider, so the Gateway performs the second exchange:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;"credentialProviderConfigurations": [{
    "credentialProviderType": "OAUTH",
    "credentialProvider": {"oauthCredentialProvider": {
        "providerArn": provider_arn,          # the JWT_AUTHORIZATION_GRANT provider from Part 1
        "scopes": ["xaa:read", "xaa:write"],  # must equal the ID-JAG's scopes
        "grantType": "TOKEN_EXCHANGE",
    }},
}]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 3 shows a target configured this way.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-3.png" alt="Gateway target for the task manager configured to redeem the ID-JAG through the Part 1 credential provider" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: The task manager target redeems the ID-JAG through the Part 1 credential provider&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Three details matter here. First, as in Part 1, the target’s scopes must equal the ID-JAG’s. Second, give each target a static tool schema (&lt;code&gt;listingMode&lt;/code&gt; set to &lt;code&gt;DEFAULT&lt;/code&gt; with an inline &lt;code&gt;mcpToolSchema&lt;/code&gt;), so the tool names your Cedar policies reference are fixed when you write them. In our tests, policies that name tools also couldn’t attach to dynamically listed targets. Third, the Gateway pages &lt;code&gt;tools/list&lt;/code&gt; by target, so your client must follow the pagination cursor, or tools from later targets disappear. AgentCore Identity keeps the token from the Gateway’s exchange, so one single-use ID-JAG serves every tool call in a session. In our tests, it also opened a second session within its 5-minute life.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because a credential provider authenticates as one Okta agent, give each agent its own Gateway and provider. Sample &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/05-authenticate-and-authorize/08-okta-xaa-delegation-chain" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;08-okta-xaa-delegation-chain&lt;/a&gt; takes a different route, in which a Gateway interceptor performs both exchanges itself.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="bind-tools-to-agents-with-cedar"&gt;Bind tools to agents with Cedar&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Attach a Policy in AgentCore engine to the Gateway in &lt;code&gt;ENFORCE&lt;/code&gt; mode, and write permit-only policies. Cedar exposes JWT claims as tags. A policy that reads a missing tag errors and is skipped. A skipped &lt;code&gt;permit&lt;/code&gt; grants nothing, so default deny holds, but a skipped &lt;code&gt;forbid&lt;/code&gt; blocks nothing. Guard each tag with &lt;code&gt;hasTag&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-cedar"&gt;permit(
  principal is AgentCore::OAuthUser,
  action in [AgentCore::Action::"taskmanager___create_task",
             AgentCore::Action::"taskmanager___update_task_status"],
  resource == AgentCore::Gateway::"&amp;lt;gateway-arn&amp;gt;"
) when {
  principal.hasTag("client_id") &amp;amp;&amp;amp; principal.getTag("client_id") == "&amp;lt;agent-client-id&amp;gt;" &amp;amp;&amp;amp;
  principal.hasTag("scope") &amp;amp;&amp;amp;
  (principal.getTag("scope") == "xaa:write" ||
   principal.getTag("scope") like "xaa:write *" ||
   principal.getTag("scope") like "* xaa:write" ||
   principal.getTag("scope") like "* xaa:write *")
};&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The scope tag holds the ID-JAG’s space-separated &lt;code&gt;scope&lt;/code&gt; claim, so the four conditions match &lt;code&gt;xaa:write&lt;/code&gt; only as a whole entry. A scope such as &lt;code&gt;xaa:write-admin&lt;/code&gt; doesn’t match. A matching policy, written the same way, permits the read tools with &lt;code&gt;xaa:read&lt;/code&gt;. Check that every policy is &lt;code&gt;ACTIVE&lt;/code&gt; before you attach the engine: an engine whose policies failed to create leaves the Gateway denying every call. Cedar binds tools to the agent and its delegated scope, and the MCP server still decides each user’s authority from the &lt;code&gt;groups&lt;/code&gt; claim, as in Part 1.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="point-the-agent-at-the-gateway"&gt;Point the agent at the Gateway&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agent A now forwards its inbound ID-JAG to the Gateway instead of calling AgentCore Identity itself:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;id_jag = context.request_headers["Authorization"].removeprefix("Bearer ")  # allowlisted header
tools = make_client(GATEWAY_URL, id_jag)  # the Gateway redeems the ID-JAG for each target&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Remove the Part 1 AgentCore Identity statements from the agent’s execution role. That’s the only change to the role, which now holds no token-exchange or secret permissions, because the Gateway’s role holds the permission to exchange tokens. Keep &lt;code&gt;allowedClients&lt;/code&gt; on the runtime’s authorizer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="run-a-second-agent-on-amazon-eks"&gt;Run a second agent on Amazon EKS&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Our second agent, a reporting agent, runs the same code on Amazon EKS with its own Okta registration. Its resource connection allows only &lt;code&gt;xaa:read&lt;/code&gt;, and it has its own Gateway and credential provider. EKS Pod Identity gives the pod an IAM role that can invoke models and write its own logs and traces, and nothing else. The AWS Distro for OpenTelemetry (ADOT) SDK exports its traces. The &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/blob/2747451473d1fadbba18f5aee0524e533e3c2b2e/03-integrations/agents-hosted-outside-runtime/agents-on-eks/deploy.sh#L137-L143" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;agents-on-eks sample&lt;/a&gt; shows the Pod Identity setup.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Outside AgentCore runtime, no authorizer sits in front of the pod, so the agent validates the ID-JAG before a model call. It checks the signature, issuer, audience, token type &lt;code&gt;oauth-id-jag+jwt&lt;/code&gt;, expiry, and that &lt;code&gt;client_id&lt;/code&gt; is its own. If you kept the Part 1 agent check in the MCP servers, add agent B’s client ID to &lt;code&gt;REGISTERED_AGENTS&lt;/code&gt;. Two settings tripped us up. The EKS Auto Mode general-purpose node pool runs only amd64, so build multi-architecture images. And the AgentCore Python SDK, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, binds to &lt;code&gt;localhost&lt;/code&gt; unless you set &lt;code&gt;DOCKER_CONTAINER=true&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="trace-every-decision-with-agentcore-observability"&gt;Trace every decision with AgentCore Observability&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The spans in this section are &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/observability.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Observability&lt;/a&gt;, the component in Figure 1 that sits between the agents and CloudWatch. Runtime, Gateway, Identity, and Policy emit OpenTelemetry spans for each hop without code changes, after you turn on Transaction Search and trace delivery. Your own services add theirs with the AWS Distro for OpenTelemetry (ADOT). CloudWatch Transaction Search collects them, the AgentCore console reads them as agent traces, and Amazon CloudWatch Omni reads the same spans as &lt;code&gt;traces.default&lt;/code&gt;, which is what lets one query put them beside Okta’s events.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One tool call therefore produces this chain of spans, each naming the control that made a decision:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Span&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Emitted by&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What it records&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;AgentCore.Gateway.InboundAuth&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Gateway&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Whether the inbound ID-JAG was issued to this agent&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;AgentCore.Policy.AuthorizeAction&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Policy&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The Cedar decision for this tool&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;AgentCore.Identity.GetWorkloadAccessToken&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Identity&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Binding the agent’s workload identity to the user&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;AgentCore.Identity.GetResourceOauth2Token&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Identity&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Redeeming the ID-JAG at Okta for a scoped access token&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;AgentCore.Gateway.InvokeTool&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Gateway&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The tool call and its outcome&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;authz.check&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The MCP server (ADOT)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The user’s groups, the agent, the decision, and the reason&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Okta’s System Log is the one record AWS can’t emit, so you bring it in. Figure 4 shows both paths meeting in Omni.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-4.png" alt="Audit path where AgentCore spans and Okta’s System Log both reach CloudWatch and one Omni query joins them" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: The audit path, where spans and Okta’s System Log reach CloudWatch and one Omni query joins them&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To set it up:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Turn on CloudWatch Transaction Search, turn on trace delivery for each Gateway (see this &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/blob/2747451473d1fadbba18f5aee0524e533e3c2b2e/02-use-cases/02-workflow-automation-agents/event-driven-claims-agent/scripts/enable_observability.py#L196-L222" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample script&lt;/a&gt;), and add ADOT to the BFF and the MCP servers. The task manager writes each decision, the user, and the agent onto an &lt;code&gt;authz.check&lt;/code&gt; span.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In Okta, add a Log Stream for Amazon EventBridge. Okta delivers each event to a partner event bus in your account, and a rule writes it to a CloudWatch Logs log group. In our tests, events arrived about 1 second after Okta published them. Each ID-JAG grant event names the agent as the actor, the user as a target, and the granted scopes and resource connection.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Set up an Omni space in the account and AWS Region, add the Okta log group to the CloudWatch Dataset integration, and save a view, &lt;code&gt;view.xaa_authz_decisions&lt;/code&gt;, over the &lt;code&gt;authz.check&lt;/code&gt; spans.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One query then joins each ID-JAG grant to the decisions it led to:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;SELECT o.okta_time, o.agent, o.user_email, o.granted, a.decision_time, a.decision, a.reason
FROM (SELECT `@timestamp` AS okta_time, detail['actor']['id'] AS agent_id,
             detail['actor']['displayName'] AS agent,
             array_element(regexp_match(`@message`, '"type"\s*:\s*"User"\s*,\s*"alternateId"\s*:\s*"([^"]+)"'), 1) AS user_email,
             detail['debugContext']['debugData']['grantedScopes'] AS granted
      FROM "logs.default"
      WHERE `@timestamp` BETWEEN NOW() - INTERVAL '1' HOUR AND NOW()
        AND detail['eventType'] = 'app.oauth2.token.grant.id_jag') o
JOIN (SELECT `@timestamp` AS decision_time, user_id, agent_client_id, decision, reason
      FROM "view.xaa_authz_decisions"
      WHERE `@timestamp` BETWEEN NOW() - INTERVAL '1' HOUR AND NOW()) a
  ON a.user_id = o.user_email AND a.agent_client_id = o.agent_id
 AND a.decision_time BETWEEN o.okta_time AND o.okta_time + INTERVAL '5' MINUTE&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For Alice, the result shows Okta granting the task agent &lt;code&gt;xaa:read xaa:write&lt;/code&gt; for her, then the MCP server denying her &lt;code&gt;create_task&lt;/code&gt; 16.7 seconds later and allowing &lt;code&gt;list_tasks&lt;/code&gt; after that, as Figure 5 shows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-5.png" alt="Omni query result joining Alice’s ID-JAG grant in Okta’s System Log to the two downstream authorization decisions" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: The Omni query joins Alice’s ID-JAG grant in Okta’s System Log to the two decisions it led to&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each request is also one trace, from the BFF through the agent, the Gateway’s identity and policy checks, and the MCP server’s decision, as Figure 6 shows. Omni marks the trace as a fault because the agent’s &lt;code&gt;create_task&lt;/code&gt; call returned the denial.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-6.png" alt="Single trace for Alice’s request from the agent’s tool call through the Gateway’s identity and Cedar checks to the MCP server’s deny" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: One trace for Alice’s request, from the agent’s tool call through the Gateway’s identity and Cedar checks to the MCP server’s deny&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two instrumentation details matter. ADOT finds the Region and signs its exports through &lt;code&gt;botocore&lt;/code&gt;, so add &lt;code&gt;botocore&lt;/code&gt; to services that lack it. Until we did, our MCP servers exported no spans. And ADOT’s agent mode turns off instrumentation for HTTP client libraries such as &lt;code&gt;requests&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;urllib3&lt;/code&gt; by default, so set &lt;code&gt;OTEL_PYTHON_DISABLED_INSTRUMENTATIONS&lt;/code&gt; in your BFF to override that default with a list that leaves out the BFF’s HTTP client (&lt;code&gt;requests&lt;/code&gt;, in ours), or its trace context won’t reach the agent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="validate-the-results"&gt;Validate the results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We ran each check through both Gateways with the users from Part 1. Each session listed the tools, then called &lt;code&gt;list_tasks&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;create_task&lt;/code&gt;, and an employee lookup:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Caller and path&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tools listed&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;create_task&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reads&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Alice, agent A, Gateway A&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;6&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Denied by the MCP server: requires &lt;code&gt;xaa-writers&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Allowed&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Bob, agent A, Gateway A&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;6&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Created&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Allowed&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Carol, agent A, Gateway A&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;6&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Denied by the MCP server: no &lt;code&gt;groups&lt;/code&gt; claim&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Allowed&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Bob, agent B, Gateway B&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;4: Cedar hid the write tools&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Refused by the Gateway: “No policy applies to the request (denied by default)”&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Allowed&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Through the deployed agents, the results matched: agent A denied Alice and Carol and created Bob’s task, and agent B told Bob it had no tool to create tasks. The same user got different authority through different agents, and Okta decided it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These requests stopped at the front door:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Request&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Response&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent B’s ID-JAG at Gateway A, or agent A’s at Gateway B&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;HTTP 403, MCP error -32002 &lt;code&gt;insufficient_scope&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;An Okta access token instead of an ID-JAG&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;HTTP 403, the same error&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No token&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;HTTP 401, “Missing Bearer token”&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent A’s ID-JAG sent to agent B on EKS&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Refused in 98 milliseconds, before a model call: “issued to a different agent”&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Gateway returns the same generic error for a wrong agent and for the wrong token type, so check your configuration against the token’s &lt;code&gt;client_id&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;aud&lt;/code&gt; when you see it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Gateway’s identity and policy work is small next to the tool itself. Medians and 90th percentiles from 24 hours of Gateway A spans in Omni:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Span&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;p50&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;p90&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Gateway.InboundAuth&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;34 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;69 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Identity.Authorize&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;25 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;60 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Identity.GetWorkloadAccessToken&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;48 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;94 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Identity.GetResourceOauth2Token&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;70 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;439 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Policy.AuthorizeAction&lt;/code&gt; (Cedar, per call)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;47 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;56 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Policy.PartiallyAuthorizeActions&lt;/code&gt; (Cedar, per tool listing)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;65 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;75 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Gateway.TargetInvocation&lt;/code&gt; (the MCP server on AgentCore runtime)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;5,565 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;6,273 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The identity and policy spans for one tool call sum to about 0.2 seconds at their medians, and the MCP server’s own &lt;code&gt;authz.check&lt;/code&gt; span took under 1 millisecond. Gateway B showed the same pattern, with &lt;code&gt;GetResourceOauth2Token&lt;/code&gt; at 408 milliseconds at the median.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cross-app-access-or-per-user-consent"&gt;Cross App Access or per-user consent&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;XAA applies where the target application’s authorization server accepts an ID-JAG. There, your Okta administrator decides which agent may act for which users, users see no consent prompt, and Okta evaluates policy each time a token is requested. Applications with their own authorization servers, such as GitHub, rely on each user’s own OAuth consent instead. Figure 7 compares the two, as we measured them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-7.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-7.png" alt="Comparison of Cross App Access and per-user OAuth consent, including the revocation behavior measured" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: Cross App Access compared with per-user OAuth consent, with the revocation behavior we measured&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We deployed the AWS sample &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/2747451473d1fadbba18f5aee0524e533e3c2b2e/01-features/05-authenticate-and-authorize/07-consent-portal-auth-code-flow-targets" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;07-consent-portal-auth-code-flow-targets&lt;/a&gt; beside our stack, with Okta signing users in to an AgentCore Identity consent portal and a GitHub target on its own Gateway. Alice connected a GitHub test account through the portal, and Bob never connected one. The post &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/manage-end-user-oauth-consent-for-ai-agents-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Manage end-user OAuth consent for AI agents with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; describes this lane in full. Here’s what we observed:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Action&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Next GitHub tool call&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Alice consented in the portal&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Returned her GitHub identity&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Bob, who never consented&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;MCP error -32042 with an AgentCore authorization URL&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Alice suspended in Okta, earlier token reused&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Still worked: the Gateway checks the inbound token’s signature and expiry, and that token lived 1 hour&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Alice suspended, new sign-in&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Refused&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Alice unsuspended&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Worked with no new consent&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Alice’s grant disconnected through the consent portal’s API&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Not connected, then -32042 again&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The two lanes revoke differently. In XAA, Okta’s controls stop the next ID-JAG mint, and issued tokens expire within about 5 minutes. In the consent lane, Okta controls only sign-in: the stored grant outlived the suspension and stayed until we disconnected it. Choose per target application, not once for the enterprise.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="where-this-pattern-applies"&gt;Where this pattern applies&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In media and entertainment, the fleet arrives quickly. A studio or streaming or games organization has agents for production tracking, title and asset metadata, rights and localization, and viewer or player support, each sitting in front of a different system with its own authorization model and its own auditors. The question isn’t whether one agent can be governed. It’s whether the hundredth one is governed the same way, and whether an administrator can still answer which agents reach which systems for which users.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Nothing here is specific to that industry. The agent is an identity in your IdP, the target is an OAuth 2.0 authorization server, and the Gateway is where authorization is decided. Teams in financial services, healthcare, and the public sector put different resources behind the same front door and get the same audit trail.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="whats-next-observability-and-evaluations"&gt;What’s next: observability and evaluations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post uses AgentCore Observability for one purpose: to prove that each allow and deny traces back to a control. There’s more in it than that, and it’s a body of work in its own right. The spans already carry the model and tool latency behind every governance decision, Transaction Search can alert on a spike in denials, and AgentCore evaluations can score an agent’s behavior against your own criteria, online for live traffic or on demand over a chosen set of spans. A later post takes that up: what to measure on a governed agent, which evaluators catch an agent that reports success after a refused tool call, and how to run both without turning your traces into your largest bill.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete the Part 2 resources, then follow the cleanup steps in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/govern-ai-agent-access-with-okta-cross-app-access-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-part-1/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Part 1&lt;/a&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the Amazon EKS cluster (&lt;code&gt;eksctl delete cluster&lt;/code&gt;) and its Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) repository.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the Gateways and their targets, then the Policy in AgentCore engines, and then agent B’s credential provider.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the CloudWatch Omni space and domain, the Dataset integration, the Amazon EventBridge rule and partner event bus, and the CloudWatch log groups.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If you deployed sample 07, follow its cleanup steps.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the IAM roles and policies you created.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In Okta, delete the log stream, and deactivate and delete agent B and its linked app.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you moved the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/govern-ai-agent-access-with-okta-cross-app-access-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-part-1/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Part 1&lt;/a&gt; solution from a topology that suits a few agents and tools to one that holds up across many. The Gateway became the single front door and redeemed Okta’s grant, so the agents held no tool credentials. Cedar policies evaluated by Policy in AgentCore replaced the check copied into each server. A second agent on Amazon EKS joined under its own Okta identity. And AgentCore Observability, with Okta’s System Log beside it in CloudWatch Omni, traced each allow and deny back to the agent, the user, and the control that decided it. The governance mechanism didn’t change, which is the point: only the topology around it did.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, explore the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/2747451473d1fadbba18f5aee0524e533e3c2b2e/01-features/05-authenticate-and-authorize" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore samples&lt;/a&gt; and Okta’s &lt;a href="https://developer.okta.com/docs/concepts/xaa/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cross App Access documentation&lt;/a&gt;. For plain token exchange through a Gateway, read &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/implement-on-behalf-of-token-exchange-for-multi-tenant-agents-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Implement on-behalf-of token exchange for multi-tenant agents with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;

  &lt;/div&gt; 
  &lt;h3 class="lb-h4"&gt;Madhu Samhitha Vangara&lt;/h3&gt; 
  &lt;p style="overflow: hidden"&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/madhu-samhitha/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Madhu&lt;/a&gt; is a Worldwide Generative AI Specialist Solution Architect at AWS, focusing on agentic AI go-to-market for Amazon Bedrock AgentCore and Strands Agents. She translates emerging AI capabilities and research into measurable outcomes for customers. Outside work, she’s a trained Indian classical dancer and an art enthusiast.&lt;/p&gt;
